People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

I even bought this ridiculous color-coded calendar—anyway, it’s kind of just been going on since then, not really letting up.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about many different things, not just school and work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. It’s not just school or work, even though those are the obvious ones. My mind kind of jumps around—like one minute I’m thinking I’m falling behind at work, and then it turns into, am I wasting all this money on school, and then suddenly I’m imagining my family thinking I can’t handle basic adult life.\n\nAnd then it’ll be something tiny, like one email I didn’t answer perfectly, and my brain treats it like that could somehow mess up my whole future. I know that sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels really real.\n\nDoctor: When you start worrying, is it hard to stop or control the worry?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s really hard to stop. I’ll try to tell myself, like, okay, focus on just one thing, just do the assignment or answer the email, but then it sort of branches off immediately.\n\nLike I’ll open my calendar to check one deadline, and then I’m checking my budget because I remembered tuition, and then I’m back to my assignments, and then I’m re-checking the calendar again. It’s like this loop where I’m doing all these “responsible” things, but I’m not actually getting anything done.\n\nDoctor: During the day, even when you’re not trying to sleep, do you still feel anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It’s not only at night or when I’m trying to sleep. During the day I’ll just be sitting at my desk and my mind starts jumping—like, am I falling behind at work, am I wasting money on school, is my family going to think I can’t handle things.\n\nAnd it can start from something so small, like one email or one deadline, and suddenly it feels like it could somehow ruin everything. I know that sounds… extreme, but my body and brain don’t seem to know the difference in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed much of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed exactly. I mean, I get really worn out and discouraged, especially when everything piles up, but then I start thinking about whether that’s sadness or just being overwhelmed, and then I’m like… maybe I’m overanalyzing even that.\n\nIt’s kind of hard to separate, honestly. Like I’ll have a bad day and then I’ll think, okay, is this a mood thing, or is it just because I spent three hours worrying about one stupid email? So… I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed feeling depressed most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often notice your muscles feeling tense or tight?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I know sometimes I’ll realize I’ve been sitting in a weird position at my desk for way too long, and then I’m like, “Is my shoulder tight because I’m stressed or because I’ve been hunched over my laptop?” \n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really paid close attention to that specifically. I notice the worrying more than the muscle part.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to concentrate?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. That’s actually one of the more frustrating things. I’ll be looking at the same financial model at work, or a reading for class, and I’ll realize I’ve gone over the same paragraph like five times and still couldn’t tell you what it said.\n\nAnd then I start thinking, okay, am I just tired, or am I distracted, or did I miss something important earlier—and then I’m not even reading anymore. My mind is already off making a list of everything that could go wrong, instead of whatever I’m supposed to be focusing on.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.   },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Yeah… it is. I kind of hate admitting that, because I keep telling myself I’m still functioning, like I’m showing up and I have spreadsheets and calendars and all these systems, but I’ve started missing deadlines at work, which is really not like me.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, mostly. I’ll get into bed and it’s like my body is tired but my brain is still at my desk, if that makes sense. I’ll have the laptop closed, lights off, everything, and I’m still mentally checking deadlines and whether I paid tuition and what assignment I’m forgetting.

Sometimes it’s stupidly specific, like I’ll remember one spreadsheet cell I might’ve messed up, and then suddenly I’m thinking about work, then my class schedule, then money, then whether I should get up and make a list—but if I get up, then I’m definitely not sleeping. So I just lie there in my apartment for hours.

And even when I do fall asleep, it doesn’t feel like I really shut off. I wake up feeling like I was still kind of working in my head all night.

So I just lie there in my apartment for hours.\n\nAnd even when I do fall asleep, it doesn’t feel like I really shut off. I wake up feeling like I was still kind of working in my head all night.\n\n[Last Question]\nIf your mind is relatively calm at bedtime, are you still unable to fall asleep?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… if my mind is calm, maybe I’d sleep better, but honestly that almost never happens lately. Bedtime is sort of when everything starts replaying.

Like I’ll be in bed in my apartment, laptop closed, trying to be “done,” and then I’m rechecking work deadlines in my head, or wondering if I paid tuition, or thinking about class assignments I might’ve missed. And then it turns into hours.

It’s not just school or work, even though those are the obvious ones. reread the same paragraph five times without absorbing it, because my head is already off running through everything that could go wrong.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I’ve started missing work deadlines and falling behind in my certificate classes, and I’ve stopped doing the routines that used to keep me grounded, like yoga, running, and cooking.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 3 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about a year and three months, since I started the evening graduate certificate program while keeping my full-time analyst job.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 29 / management_analyst / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S027\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you been feeling down or depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say depressed nearly every day. I mean, I have days where I feel really worn down or kind of… flat, I guess, but then I start thinking maybe that’s just from everything piling up.\n\nIt’s hard to separate out, because if I have a bad morning, then I’ll spend the whole commute replaying it, and then I’m late answering emails, and then suddenly the day feels ruined. But I don’t know if that’s the same as being depressed most of the day. I haven’t really been tracking it that way.\n\nDoctor: When you can’t sleep, is it usually because your mind is worrying or racing?\n\nPatient: Yeah, mostly. I’ll get into bed and it’s like my body is tired but my brain is still at my desk, if that makes sense. I’ll have the laptop closed, lights off, everything, and I’m still mentally checking deadlines and whether I paid tuition and what assignment I’m forgetting.\n\nSometimes it’s stupidly specific, like I’ll remember one spreadsheet cell I might’ve messed up, and then suddenly I’m thinking about work, then my class schedule, then money, then whether I should get up and make a list—but if I get up, then I’m definitely not sleeping. So I just lie there in my apartment for hours.\n\nAnd even when I do fall asleep, it doesn’t feel like I really shut off. I wake up feeling like I was still kind of working in my head all night.\n\nDoctor: If your mind is relatively calm at bedtime, are you still unable to fall asleep?\n\nPatient: I mean… if my mind is calm, maybe I’d sleep better, but honestly that almost never happens lately. Bedtime is sort of when everything starts replaying.\n\nLike I’ll be in bed in my apartment, laptop closed, trying to be “done,” and then I’m rechecking work deadlines in my head, or wondering if I paid tuition, or thinking about class assignments I might’ve missed. And then it turns into hours.\n\nSo I don’t know if I can really answer for a calm night, because the problem is that I don’t really get many of those. Even when I do fall asleep, I wake up feeling like I never actually turned off.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been worrying like this on most days for at least six months?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Um… honestly, my sleep is the main reason I finally made the appointment. It’s kind of fallen apart. I’ll be in bed in my apartment for hours, like my laptop is already closed and I’m trying to be done for the night, but my brain is still going through work deadlines and tuition payments and class assignments—just rechecking everything like I’m going to catch something I missed.\n\nAnd it’s stupid because sometimes I’ll tell myself, “Okay, just sleep,” but then I remember some email or a due date, and then I’m back in it. Even when I do fall asleep, I wake up feeling like I never really turned off. Like I was technically asleep, but not rested.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after a specific stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it was one specific event, exactly. It’s more like it started when I began this evening graduate certificate program while still working full-time as an analyst. That was… about a year and three months ago now, I think.\n\nAt first I thought it was just normal adjustment, like everyone’s busy and I just needed a better planner or something. I even bought this ridiculous color-coded calendar—anyway, it’s kind of just been going on since then, not really letting up.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about many different things, not just school and work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. It’s not just school or work, even though those are the obvious ones. My mind kind of jumps around—like one minute I’m thinking I’m falling behind at work, and then it turns into, am I wasting all this money on school, and then suddenly I’m imagining my family thinking I can’t handle basic adult life.\n\nAnd then it’ll be something tiny, like one email I didn’t answer perfectly, and my brain treats it like that could somehow mess up my whole future. I know that sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels really real.\n\nDoctor: When you start worrying, is it hard to stop or control the worry?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s really hard to stop. I’ll try to tell myself, like, okay, focus on just one thing, just do the assignment or answer the email, but then it sort of branches off immediately.\n\nLike I’ll open my calendar to check one deadline, and then I’m checking my budget because I remembered tuition, and then I’m back to my assignments, and then I’m re-checking the calendar again. It’s like this loop where I’m doing all these “responsible” things, but I’m not actually getting anything done.\n\nDoctor: During the day, even when you’re not trying to sleep, do you still feel anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It’s not only at night or when I’m trying to sleep. During the day I’ll just be sitting at my desk and my mind starts jumping—like, am I falling behind at work, am I wasting money on school, is my family going to think I can’t handle things.\n\nAnd it can start from something so small, like one email or one deadline, and suddenly it feels like it could somehow ruin everything. I know that sounds… extreme, but my body and brain don’t seem to know the difference in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed much of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed exactly. I mean, I get really worn out and discouraged, especially when everything piles up, but then I start thinking about whether that’s sadness or just being overwhelmed, and then I’m like… maybe I’m overanalyzing even that.\n\nIt’s kind of hard to separate, honestly. Like I’ll have a bad day and then I’ll think, okay, is this a mood thing, or is it just because I spent three hours worrying about one stupid email? So… I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed feeling depressed most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often notice your muscles feeling tense or tight?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I know sometimes I’ll realize I’ve been sitting in a weird position at my desk for way too long, and then I’m like, “Is my shoulder tight because I’m stressed or because I’ve been hunched over my laptop?” \n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really paid close attention to that specifically. I notice the worrying more than the muscle part.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to concentrate?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. That’s actually one of the more frustrating things. I’ll be looking at the same financial model at work, or a reading for class, and I’ll realize I’ve gone over the same paragraph like five times and still couldn’t tell you what it said.\n\nAnd then I start thinking, okay, am I just tired, or am I distracted, or did I miss something important earlier—and then I’m not even reading anymore. My mind is already off making a list of everything that could go wrong, instead of whatever I’m supposed to be focusing on.\n\nDoctor: Have you been more irritable than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I can get snappy sometimes, but then I start wondering if that’s actually irritability or if I’m just reacting to whatever’s happening in the moment. Like, at work, if someone sends three follow-up messages before I’ve even had time to answer the first one, I’ll feel this flash of annoyance—but then who wouldn’t?\n\nI guess I haven’t tracked whether it’s more than usual. My usual is kind of hard to compare to, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Is this worrying interfering with your work, school, or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I kind of hate admitting that, because I keep telling myself I’m still functioning, like I’m showing up and I have spreadsheets and calendars and all these systems, but I’ve started missing deadlines at work, which is really not like me.\n\nAnd with my certificate classes, I’m falling behind there too. I’ll mean to catch up on lectures or assignments, and then somehow the whole evening turns into me worrying about being behind instead of actually doing the work. It’s ridiculous.\n\nI’ve also stopped doing the things that used to keep me sort of steady, like yoga and running and cooking decent meals. I used to be pretty consistent with that stuff—well, not perfect, but you know, I had routines. Now I’ll buy groceries and then just… not cook, or I’ll plan to run and then spend an hour thinking about everything I need to fix first. So yes, I think it’s interfering.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you been feeling down or depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say depressed nearly every day. I mean, I have days where I feel really worn down or kind of… flat, I guess, but then I start thinking maybe that’s just from everything piling up.\n\nIt’s hard to separate out, because if I have a bad morning, then I’ll spend the whole commute replaying it, and then I’m late answering emails, and then suddenly the day feels ruined. But I don’t know if that’s the same as being depressed most of the day. I haven’t really been tracking it that way.\n\nDoctor: When you can’t sleep, is it usually because your mind is worrying or racing?\n\nPatient: Yeah, mostly. I wake up feeling like I was still kind of working in my head all night.\n\nDoctor: If your mind is relatively calm at bedtime, are you still unable to fall asleep?\n\nPatient: I mean… if my mind is calm, maybe I’d sleep better, but honestly that almost never happens lately. I’m not totally sure how to separate “restless” from just… busy, if that makes sense. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Um… honestly, my sleep is the main reason I finally made the appointment. It’s kind of fallen apart. I’ll be in bed in my apartment for hours, like my laptop is already closed and I’m trying to be done for the night, but my brain is still going through work deadlines and tuition payments and class assignments—just rechecking everything like I’m going to catch something I missed.\n\nAnd it’s stupid because sometimes I’ll tell myself, “Okay, just sleep,” but then I remember some email or a due date, and then I’m back in it. Even when I do fall asleep, I wake up feeling like I never really turned off. Like I was technically asleep, but not rested.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after a specific stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it was one specific event, exactly. It’s more like it started when I began this evening graduate certificate program while still working full-time as an analyst. That was… about a year and three months ago now, I think.\n\nAt first I thought it was just normal adjustment, like everyone’s busy and I just needed a better planner or something. I even bought this ridiculous color-coded calendar—anyway, it’s kind of just been going on since then, not really letting up.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about many different things, not just school and work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. It’s not just school or work, even though those are the obvious ones. My mind kind of jumps around—like one minute I’m thinking I’m falling behind at work, and then it turns into, am I wasting all this money on school, and then suddenly I’m imagining my family thinking I can’t handle basic adult life.\n\nAnd then it’ll be something tiny, like one email I didn’t answer perfectly, and my brain treats it like that could somehow mess up my whole future. I know that sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels really real.\n\nDoctor: When you start worrying, is it hard to stop or control the worry?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s really hard to stop. I’ll try to tell myself, like, okay, focus on just one thing, just do the assignment or answer the email, but then it sort of branches off immediately.\n\nLike I’ll open my calendar to check one deadline, and then I’m checking my budget because I remembered tuition, and then I’m back to my assignments, and then I’m re-checking the calendar again. It’s like this loop where I’m doing all these “responsible” things, but I’m not actually getting anything done.\n\nDoctor: During the day, even when you’re not trying to sleep, do you still feel anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It’s not only at night or when I’m trying to sleep. During the day I’ll just be sitting at my desk and my mind starts jumping—like, am I falling behind at work, am I wasting money on school, is my family going to think I can’t handle things.\n\nAnd it can start from something so small, like one email or one deadline, and suddenly it feels like it could somehow ruin everything. I know that sounds… extreme, but my body and brain don’t seem to know the difference in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed much of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed exactly. I mean, I get really worn out and discouraged, especially when everything piles up, but then I start thinking about whether that’s sadness or just being overwhelmed, and then I’m like… maybe I’m overanalyzing even that.\n\nIt’s kind of hard to separate, honestly. Like I’ll have a bad day and then I’ll think, okay, is this a mood thing, or is it just because I spent three hours worrying about one stupid email? So… I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed feeling depressed most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often notice your muscles feeling tense or tight?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I know sometimes I’ll realize I’ve been sitting in a weird position at my desk for way too long, and then I’m like, “Is my shoulder tight because I’m stressed or because I’ve been hunched over my laptop?” \n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really paid close attention to that specifically. I notice the worrying more than the muscle part.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to concentrate?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. That’s actually one of the more frustrating things. I’ll be looking at the same financial model at work, or a reading for class, and I’ll realize I’ve gone over the same paragraph like five times and still couldn’t tell you what it said.\n\nAnd then I start thinking, okay, am I just tired, or am I distracted, or did I miss something important earlier—and then I’m not even reading anymore. My mind is already off making a list of everything that could go wrong, instead of whatever I’m supposed to be focusing on.\n\nDoctor: Have you been more irritable than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I can get snappy sometimes, but then I start wondering if that’s actually irritability or if I’m just reacting to whatever’s happening in the moment. Like, at work, if someone sends three follow-up messages before I’ve even had time to answer the first one, I’ll feel this flash of annoyance—but then who wouldn’t?\n\nI guess I haven’t tracked whether it’s more than usual. My usual is kind of hard to compare to, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Is this worrying interfering with your work, school, or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I kind of hate admitting that, because I keep telling myself I’m still functioning, like I’m showing up and I have spreadsheets and calendars and all these systems, but I’ve started missing deadlines at work, which is really not like me.\n\nAnd with my certificate classes, I’m falling behind there too. I’ll mean to catch up on lectures or assignments, and then somehow the whole evening turns into me worrying about being behind instead of actually doing the work. It’s ridiculous.\n\nI’ve also stopped doing the things that used to keep me sort of steady, like yoga and running and cooking decent meals. I used to be pretty consistent with that stuff—well, not perfect, but you know, I had routines. Now I’ll buy groceries and then just… not cook, or I’ll plan to run and then spend an hour thinking about everything I need to fix first. So yes, I think it’s interfering.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you been feeling down or depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say depressed nearly every day. I mean, I have days where I feel really worn down or kind of… flat, I guess, but then I start thinking maybe that’s just from everything piling up.\n\nIt’s hard to separate out, because if I have a bad morning, then I’ll spend the whole commute replaying it, and then I’m late answering emails, and then suddenly the day feels ruined. But I don’t know if that’s the same as being depressed most of the day. I’ll mean to catch up on lectures or assignments, and then somehow the whole evening turns into me worrying about being behind instead of actually doing the work. It’s ridiculous.\n\nI’ve also stopped doing the things that used to keep me sort of steady, like yoga and running and cooking decent meals. I used to be pretty consistent with that stuff—well, not perfect, but you know, I had routines. Now I’ll buy groceries and then just… not cook, or I’ll plan to run and then spend an hour thinking about everything I need to fix first. So yes, I think it’s interfering.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you been feeling down or depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say depressed nearly every day. I mean, I have days where I feel really worn down or kind of… flat, I guess, but then I start thinking maybe that’s just from everything piling up.\n\nIt’s hard to separate out, because if I have a bad morning, then I’ll spend the whole commute replaying it, and then I’m late answering emails, and then suddenly the day feels ruined. But I don’t know if that’s the same as being depressed most of the day. I haven’t really been tracking it that way.\n\nDoctor: When you can’t sleep, is it usually because your mind is worrying or racing?\n\nPatient: Yeah, mostly. I’ll get into bed and it’s like my body is tired but my brain is still at my desk, if that makes sense. I’ll have the laptop closed, lights off, everything, and I’m still mentally checking deadlines and whether I paid tuition and what assignment I’m forgetting.\n\nSometimes it’s stupidly specific, like I’ll remember one spreadsheet cell I might’ve messed up, and then suddenly I’m thinking about work, then my class schedule, then money, then whether I should get up and make a list—but if I get up, then I’m definitely not sleeping. So I just lie there in my apartment for hours.\n\nAnd even when I do fall asleep, it doesn’t feel like I really shut off. I wake up feeling like I was still kind of working in my head all night.\n\nDoctor: If your mind is relatively calm at bedtime, are you still unable to fall asleep?\n\nPatient: I mean… if my mind is calm, maybe I’d sleep better, but honestly that almost never happens lately. Bedtime is sort of when everything starts replaying.\n\nLike I’ll be in bed in my apartment, laptop closed, trying to be “done,” and then I’m rechecking work deadlines in my head, or wondering if I paid tuition, or thinking about class assignments I might’ve missed. And then it turns into hours.\n\nSo I don’t know if I can really answer for a calm night, because the problem is that I don’t really get many of those. Even when I do fall asleep, I wake up feeling like I never actually turned off.\n\nDoctor: Have you been worrying like this on most days for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s definitely been more than six months. I’d say about a year and a few months—maybe a year and three months? It really started when I began that evening graduate certificate program while still working full-time as a management analyst.\n\nAt first I thought it was just because the semester was new and I needed to get organized, like buying the right planner and figuring out which nights were for class stuff versus work stuff. But it just kind of… kept going. So, yes, most days for that whole stretch.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or unable to relax?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate “restless” from just… busy, if that makes sense. I do have a hard time just sitting and relaxing, but then I also feel like everyone I know is kind of like that now—like my roommate will put on a show and still be scrolling and texting three people, so I don’t know what counts as abnormal.\n\nI guess if I try to relax, I’m usually thinking of what else I should be doing. Even on the couch I’ll sort of pop up to check something or move around the apartment. But, yeah, I don’t know if I’d call it restless exactly. What do you mean by unable to relax?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. That was… about a year and three months ago now, I think.\n\nAt first I thought it was just normal adjustment, like everyone’s busy and I just needed a better planner or something. I even bought this ridiculous color-coded calendar—anyway, it’s kind of just been going on since then, not really letting up.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about many different things, not just school and work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. It’s not just school or work, even though those are the obvious ones. My mind kind of jumps around—like one minute I’m thinking I’m falling behind at work, and then it turns into, am I wasting all this money on school, and then suddenly I’m imagining my family thinking I can’t handle basic adult life.\n\nAnd then it’ll be something tiny, like one email I didn’t answer perfectly, and my brain treats it like that could somehow mess up my whole future. I know that sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels really real.\n\nDoctor: When you start worrying, is it hard to stop or control the worry?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s really hard to stop. I’ll try to tell myself, like, okay, focus on just one thing, just do the assignment or answer the email, but then it sort of branches off immediately.\n\nLike I’ll open my calendar to check one deadline, and then I’m checking my budget because I remembered tuition, and then I’m back to my assignments, and then I’m re-checking the calendar again. It’s like this loop where I’m doing all these “responsible” things, but I’m not actually getting anything done.\n\nDoctor: During the day, even when you’re not trying to sleep, do you still feel anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It’s not only at night or when I’m trying to sleep. During the day I’ll just be sitting at my desk and my mind starts jumping—like, am I falling behind at work, am I wasting money on school, is my family going to think I can’t handle things.\n\nAnd it can start from something so small, like one email or one deadline, and suddenly it feels like it could somehow ruin everything. I know that sounds… extreme, but my body and brain don’t seem to know the difference in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed much of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed exactly. I mean, I get really worn out and discouraged, especially when everything piles up, but then I start thinking about whether that’s sadness or just being overwhelmed, and then I’m like… maybe I’m overanalyzing even that.\n\nIt’s kind of hard to separate, honestly. Like I’ll have a bad day and then I’ll think, okay, is this a mood thing, or is it just because I spent three hours worrying about one stupid email? So… I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed feeling depressed most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often notice your muscles feeling tense or tight?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I know sometimes I’ll realize I’ve been sitting in a weird position at my desk for way too long, and then I’m like, “Is my shoulder tight because I’m stressed or because I’ve been hunched over my laptop?” \n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really paid close attention to that specifically. I notice the worrying more than the muscle part.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to concentrate?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. That’s actually one of the more frustrating things. I’ll be looking at the same financial model at work, or a reading for class, and I’ll realize I’ve gone over the same paragraph like five times and still couldn’t tell you what it said.\n\nAnd then I start thinking, okay, am I just tired, or am I distracted, or did I miss something important earlier—and then I’m not even reading anymore. My mind is already off making a list of everything that could go wrong, instead of whatever I’m supposed to be focusing on.\n\nDoctor: Have you been more irritable than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I can get snappy sometimes, but then I start wondering if that’s actually irritability or if I’m just reacting to whatever’s happening in the moment. Like, at work, if someone sends three follow-up messages before I’ve even had time to answer the first one, I’ll feel this flash of annoyance—but then who wouldn’t?\n\nI guess I haven’t tracked whether it’s more than usual. My usual is kind of hard to compare to, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Is this worrying interfering with your work, school, or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I kind of hate admitting that, because I keep telling myself I’m still functioning, like I’m showing up and I have spreadsheets and calendars and all these systems, but I’ve started missing deadlines at work, which is really not like me.\n\nAnd with my certificate classes, I’m falling behind there too. I’ll mean to catch up on lectures or assignments, and then somehow the whole evening turns into me worrying about being behind instead of actually doing the work. It’s ridiculous.\n\nI’ve also stopped doing the things that used to keep me sort of steady, like yoga and running and cooking decent meals. I used to be pretty consistent with that stuff—well, not perfect, but you know, I had routines. Now I’ll buy groceries and then just… not cook, or I’ll plan to run and then spend an hour thinking about everything I need to fix first. So yes, I think it’s interfering.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you been feeling down or depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say depressed nearly every day. I mean, I have days where I feel really worn down or kind of… flat, I guess, but then I start thinking maybe that’s just from everything piling up.\n\nIt’s hard to separate out, because if I have a bad morning, then I’ll spend the whole commute replaying it, and then I’m late answering emails, and then suddenly the day feels ruined. But I don’t know if that’s the same as being depressed most of the day. I haven’t really been tracking it that way.\n\nDoctor: When you can’t sleep, is it usually because your mind is worrying or racing?\n\nPatient: Yeah, mostly. I’ll get into bed and it’s like my body is tired but my brain is still at my desk, if that makes sense. I’ll have the laptop closed, lights off, everything, and I’m still mentally checking deadlines and whether I paid tuition and what assignment I’m forgetting.\n\nSometimes it’s stupidly specific, like I’ll remember one spreadsheet cell I might’ve messed up, and then suddenly I’m thinking about work, then my class schedule, then money, then whether I should get up and make a list—but if I get up, then I’m definitely not sleeping. So I just lie there in my apartment for hours.\n\nAnd even when I do fall asleep, it doesn’t feel like I really shut off. I wake up feeling like I was still kind of working in my head all night.\n\nDoctor: If your mind is relatively calm at bedtime, are you still unable to fall asleep?\n\nPatient: I mean… if my mind is calm, maybe I’d sleep better, but honestly that almost never happens lately. Bedtime is sort of when everything starts replaying.\n\nLike I’ll be in bed in my apartment, laptop closed, trying to be “done,” and then I’m rechecking work deadlines in my head, or wondering if I paid tuition, or thinking about class assignments I might’ve missed. And then it turns into hours.\n\nSo I don’t know if I can really answer for a calm night, because the problem is that I don’t really get many of those. Even when I do fall asleep, I wake up feeling like I never actually turned off.\n\nDoctor: Have you been worrying like this on most days for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s definitely been more than six months. I’d say about a year and a few months—maybe a year and three months? It really started when I began that evening graduate certificate program while still working full-time as a management analyst.\n\nAt first I thought it was just because the semester was new and I needed to get organized, like buying the right planner and figuring out which nights were for class stuff versus work stuff. But it just kind of… kept going. So, yes, most days for that whole stretch.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or unable to relax?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate “restless” from just… busy, if that makes sense. I do have a hard time just sitting and relaxing, but then I also feel like everyone I know is kind of like that now—like my roommate will put on a show and still be scrolling and texting three people, so I don’t know what counts as abnormal.\n\nI guess if I try to relax, I’m usually thinking of what else I should be doing. Even on the couch I’ll sort of pop up to check something or move around the apartment. But, yeah, I don’t know if I’d call it restless exactly. What do you mean by unable to relax?\n\nDoctor: On nights when you’re not worrying much, do you still have trouble falling asleep?\n\nPatient: I guess… I don’t really have many nights where I’m not worrying much, so it’s hard to separate out. Like, by the time I’m in bed, even if I closed my laptop hours ago, my brain starts doing this checklist thing—work deadlines, whether I paid tuition, class assignments I might’ve forgotten.\n\nSo the sleep part has basically fallen apart. I’ll just lie there in my apartment for hours, and then if I do finally sleep, I wake up feeling like I never actually shut off. I’m sorry, I know that doesn’t exactly answer “on nights when I’m not worrying,” but those nights are kind of rare for me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I wake up feeling like I was still kind of working in my head all night.\n\nDoctor: If your mind is relatively calm at bedtime, are you still unable to fall asleep?\n\nPatient: I mean… if my mind is calm, maybe I’d sleep better, but honestly that almost never happens lately. Even on the couch I’ll sort of pop up to check something or move around the apartment. But, yeah, I don’t know if I’d call it restless exactly. What do you mean by unable to relax?\n\nDoctor: On nights when you’re not worrying much, do you still have trouble falling asleep?\n\nPatient: I guess… I don’t really have many nights where I’m not worrying much, so it’s hard to separate out. I’ll be in bed in my apartment for hours, like my laptop is already closed and I’m trying to be done for the night, but my brain is still going through work deadlines and tuition payments and class assignments—just rechecking everything like I’m going to catch something I missed.\n\nAnd it’s stupid because sometimes I’ll tell myself, “Okay, just sleep,” but then I remember some email or a due date, and then I’m back in it. Even when I do fall asleep, I wake up feeling like I never really turned off. Like I was technically asleep, but not rested.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after a specific stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it was one specific event, exactly. It’s more like it started when I began this evening graduate certificate program while still working full-time as an analyst. That was… about a year and three months ago now, I think.\n\nAt first I thought it was just normal adjustment, like everyone’s busy and I just needed a better planner or something. I even bought this ridiculous color-coded calendar—anyway, it’s kind of just been going on since then, not really letting up.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about many different things, not just school and work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. It’s not just school or work, even though those are the obvious ones. My mind kind of jumps around—like one minute I’m thinking I’m falling behind at work, and then it turns into, am I wasting all this money on school, and then suddenly I’m imagining my family thinking I can’t handle basic adult life.\n\nAnd then it’ll be something tiny, like one email I didn’t answer perfectly, and my brain treats it like that could somehow mess up my whole future. I know that sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels really real.\n\nDoctor: When you start worrying, is it hard to stop or control the worry?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s really hard to stop. I’ll try to tell myself, like, okay, focus on just one thing, just do the assignment or answer the email, but then it sort of branches off immediately.\n\nLike I’ll open my calendar to check one deadline, and then I’m checking my budget because I remembered tuition, and then I’m back to my assignments, and then I’m re-checking the calendar again. It’s like this loop where I’m doing all these “responsible” things, but I’m not actually getting anything done.\n\nDoctor: During the day, even when you’re not trying to sleep, do you still feel anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It’s not only at night or when I’m trying to sleep. During the day I’ll just be sitting at my desk and my mind starts jumping—like, am I falling behind at work, am I wasting money on school, is my family going to think I can’t handle things.\n\nAnd it can start from something so small, like one email or one deadline, and suddenly it feels like it could somehow ruin everything. I know that sounds… extreme, but my body and brain don’t seem to know the difference in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed much of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed exactly. I mean, I get really worn out and discouraged, especially when everything piles up, but then I start thinking about whether that’s sadness or just being overwhelmed, and then I’m like… maybe I’m overanalyzing even that.\n\nIt’s kind of hard to separate, honestly. Like I’ll have a bad day and then I’ll think, okay, is this a mood thing, or is it just because I spent three hours worrying about one stupid email? So… I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed feeling depressed most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often notice your muscles feeling tense or tight?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I know sometimes I’ll realize I’ve been sitting in a weird position at my desk for way too long, and then I’m like, “Is my shoulder tight because I’m stressed or because I’ve been hunched over my laptop?” \n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really paid close attention to that specifically. I notice the worrying more than the muscle part.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to concentrate?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. That’s actually one of the more frustrating things. I’ll be looking at the same financial model at work, or a reading for class, and I’ll realize I’ve gone over the same paragraph like five times and still couldn’t tell you what it said.\n\nAnd then I start thinking, okay, am I just tired, or am I distracted, or did I miss something important earlier—and then I’m not even reading anymore. My mind is already off making a list of everything that could go wrong, instead of whatever I’m supposed to be focusing on.\n\nDoctor: Have you been more irritable than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I can get snappy sometimes, but then I start wondering if that’s actually irritability or if I’m just reacting to whatever’s happening in the moment. Like, at work, if someone sends three follow-up messages before I’ve even had time to answer the first one, I’ll feel this flash of annoyance—but then who wouldn’t?\n\nI guess I haven’t tracked whether it’s more than usual. My usual is kind of hard to compare to, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Is this worrying interfering with your work, school, or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I kind of hate admitting that, because I keep telling myself I’m still functioning, like I’m showing up and I have spreadsheets and calendars and all these systems, but I’ve started missing deadlines at work, which is really not like me.\n\nAnd with my certificate classes, I’m falling behind there too. I’ll mean to catch up on lectures or assignments, and then somehow the whole evening turns into me worrying about being behind instead of actually doing the work. It’s ridiculous.\n\nI’ve also stopped doing the things that used to keep me sort of steady, like yoga and running and cooking decent meals. I used to be pretty consistent with that stuff—well, not perfect, but you know, I had routines. Now I’ll buy groceries and then just… not cook, or I’ll plan to run and then spend an hour thinking about everything I need to fix first. So yes, I think it’s interfering.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you been feeling down or depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say depressed nearly every day. I mean, I have days where I feel really worn down or kind of… flat, I guess, but then I start thinking maybe that’s just from everything piling up.\n\nIt’s hard to separate out, because if I have a bad morning, then I’ll spend the whole commute replaying it, and then I’m late answering emails, and then suddenly the day feels ruined. But I don’t know if that’s the same as being depressed most of the day. I haven’t really been tracking it that way.\n\nDoctor: When you can’t sleep, is it usually because your mind is worrying or racing?\n\nPatient: Yeah, mostly. I’ll get into bed and it’s like my body is tired but my brain is still at my desk, if that makes sense. I’ll have the laptop closed, lights off, everything, and I’m still mentally checking deadlines and whether I paid tuition and what assignment I’m forgetting.\n\nSometimes it’s stupidly specific, like I’ll remember one spreadsheet cell I might’ve messed up, and then suddenly I’m thinking about work, then my class schedule, then money, then whether I should get up and make a list—but if I get up, then I’m definitely not sleeping. So I just lie there in my apartment for hours.\n\nAnd even when I do fall asleep, it doesn’t feel like I really shut off. I wake up feeling like I was still kind of working in my head all night.\n\nDoctor: If your mind is relatively calm at bedtime, are you still unable to fall asleep?\n\nPatient: I mean… if my mind is calm, maybe I’d sleep better, but honestly that almost never happens lately. Bedtime is sort of when everything starts replaying.\n\nLike I’ll be in bed in my apartment, laptop closed, trying to be “done,” and then I’m rechecking work deadlines in my head, or wondering if I paid tuition, or thinking about class assignments I might’ve missed. And then it turns into hours.\n\nSo I don’t know if I can really answer for a calm night, because the problem is that I don’t really get many of those. Even when I do fall asleep, I wake up feeling like I never actually turned off.\n\nDoctor: Have you been worrying like this on most days for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s definitely been more than six months. I’d say about a year and a few months—maybe a year and three months? It really started when I began that evening graduate certificate program while still working full-time as a management analyst.\n\nAt first I thought it was just because the semester was new and I needed to get organized, like buying the right planner and figuring out which nights were for class stuff versus work stuff. But it just kind of… kept going. So, yes, most days for that whole stretch.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or unable to relax?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate “restless” from just… busy, if that makes sense. I do have a hard time just sitting and relaxing, but then I also feel like everyone I know is kind of like that now—like my roommate will put on a show and still be scrolling and texting three people, so I don’t know what counts as abnormal.\n\nI guess if I try to relax, I’m usually thinking of what else I should be doing. Even on the couch I’ll sort of pop up to check something or move around the apartment. But, yeah, I don’t know if I’d call it restless exactly. What do you mean by unable to relax?\n\nDoctor: On nights when you’re not worrying much, do you still have trouble falling asleep?\n\nPatient: I guess… I don’t really have many nights where I’m not worrying much, so it’s hard to separate out. Like, by the time I’m in bed, even if I closed my laptop hours ago, my brain starts doing this checklist thing—work deadlines, whether I paid tuition, class assignments I might’ve forgotten.\n\nSo the sleep part has basically fallen apart. I’ll just lie there in my apartment for hours, and then if I do finally sleep, I wake up feeling like I never actually shut off. I’m sorry, I know that doesn’t exactly answer “on nights when I’m not worrying,” but those nights are kind of rare for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really thought about it that way. I still do things I usually like, or I mean, I try to—like I’ll put something on or make plans and then halfway through I’m thinking about whether I responded to an email or if I’m forgetting something.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say I’ve completely stopped enjoying things? It’s more like I’m distracted. Like my friend wanted to go to this new coffee place and I spent half the time looking at the menu and then thinking about how I should reorganize my calendar, which is ridiculous because we were just getting coffee. So… I’m not sure. Maybe less present, but I don’t know if that’s the same as losing interest.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you had times when you felt hopeless?\n\nPatient: Hopeless? I’m not totally sure. I don’t think I’d use that word exactly… like, I get overwhelmed and I can spiral about things, but “hopeless” feels kind of big.\n\nAlthough, I guess sometimes when people ask that, I start thinking about my cousin who used to say everything was “hopeless” if she couldn’t find parking, so I’m not sure if we’re using it that way or in a more serious way. Over the past couple weeks, I don’t know that I’ve noticed feeling like there’s no point or anything. More just… stuck in my head, if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

  },

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Um… honestly, my sleep is the main reason I finally made the appointment. It’s kind of fallen apart. I’ll be in bed in my apartment for hours, like my laptop is already closed and I’m trying to be done for the night, but my brain is still going through work deadlines and tuition payments and class assignments—just rechecking everything like I’m going to catch something I missed.\n\nAnd it’s stupid because sometimes I’ll tell myself, “Okay, just sleep,” but then I remember some email or a due date, and then I’m back in it. Even when I do fall asleep, I wake up feeling like I never really turned off. Like I was technically asleep, but not rested.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after a specific stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it was one specific event, exactly. It’s more like it started when I began this evening graduate certificate program while still working full-time as an analyst. That was… about a year and three months ago now, I think.\n\nAt first I thought it was just normal adjustment, like everyone’s busy and I just needed a better planner or something. I even bought this ridiculous color-coded calendar—anyway, it’s kind of just been going on since then, not really letting up.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about many different things, not just school and work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. It’s not just school or work, even though those are the obvious ones. My mind kind of jumps around—like one minute I’m thinking I’m falling behind at work, and then it turns into, am I wasting all this money on school, and then suddenly I’m imagining my family thinking I can’t handle basic adult life.\n\nAnd then it’ll be something tiny, like one email I didn’t answer perfectly, and my brain treats it like that could somehow mess up my whole future. I know that sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels really real.\n\nDoctor: When you start worrying, is it hard to stop or control the worry?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s really hard to stop. I’ll try to tell myself, like, okay, focus on just one thing, just do the assignment or answer the email, but then it sort of branches off immediately.\n\nLike I’ll open my calendar to check one deadline, and then I’m checking my budget because I remembered tuition, and then I’m back to my assignments, and then I’m re-checking the calendar again. It’s like this loop where I’m doing all these “responsible” things, but I’m not actually getting anything done.\n\nDoctor: During the day, even when you’re not trying to sleep, do you still feel anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It’s not only at night or when I’m trying to sleep. During the day I’ll just be sitting at my desk and my mind starts jumping—like, am I falling behind at work, am I wasting money on school, is my family going to think I can’t handle things.\n\nAnd it can start from something so small, like one email or one deadline, and suddenly it feels like it could somehow ruin everything. I know that sounds… extreme, but my body and brain don’t seem to know the difference in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed much of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed exactly. I mean, I get really worn out and discouraged, especially when everything piles up, but then I start thinking about whether that’s sadness or just being overwhelmed, and then I’m like… maybe I’m overanalyzing even that.\n\nIt’s kind of hard to separate, honestly. Like I’ll have a bad day and then I’ll think, okay, is this a mood thing, or is it just because I spent three hours worrying about one stupid email? So… I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed feeling depressed most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often notice your muscles feeling tense or tight?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I know sometimes I’ll realize I’ve been sitting in a weird position at my desk for way too long, and then I’m like, “Is my shoulder tight because I’m stressed or because I’ve been hunched over my laptop?” \n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really paid close attention to that specifically. I notice the worrying more than the muscle part.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to concentrate?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. That’s actually one of the more frustrating things. I’ll be looking at the same financial model at work, or a reading for class, and I’ll realize I’ve gone over the same paragraph like five times and still couldn’t tell you what it said.\n\nAnd then I start thinking, okay, am I just tired, or am I distracted, or did I miss something important earlier—and then I’m not even reading anymore. My mind is already off making a list of everything that could go wrong, instead of whatever I’m supposed to be focusing on.\n\nDoctor: Have you been more irritable than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I can get snappy sometimes, but then I start wondering if that’s actually irritability or if I’m just reacting to whatever’s happening in the moment. Like, at work, if someone sends three follow-up messages before I’ve even had time to answer the first one, I’ll feel this flash of annoyance—but then who wouldn’t?\n\nI guess I haven’t tracked whether it’s more than usual. My usual is kind of hard to compare to, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Is this worrying interfering with your work, school, or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I kind of hate admitting that, because I keep telling myself I’m still functioning, like I’m showing up and I have spreadsheets and calendars and all these systems, but I’ve started missing deadlines at work, which is really not like me.\n\nAnd with my certificate classes, I’m falling behind there too. I’ll mean to catch up on lectures or assignments, and then somehow the whole evening turns into me worrying about being behind instead of actually doing the work. It’s ridiculous.\n\nI’ve also stopped doing the things that used to keep me sort of steady, like yoga and running and cooking decent meals. I used to be pretty consistent with that stuff—well, not perfect, but you know, I had routines. Now I’ll buy groceries and then just… not cook, or I’ll plan to run and then spend an hour thinking about everything I need to fix first. So yes, I think it’s interfering.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you been feeling down or depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say depressed nearly every day. I mean, I have days where I feel really worn down or kind of… flat, I guess, but then I start thinking maybe that’s just from everything piling up.\n\nIt’s hard to separate out, because if I have a bad morning, then I’ll spend the whole commute replaying it, and then I’m late answering emails, and then suddenly the day feels ruined. But I don’t know if that’s the same as being depressed most of the day. I haven’t really been tracking it that way.\n\nDoctor: When you can’t sleep, is it usually because your mind is worrying or racing?\n\nPatient: Yeah, mostly. I’ll get into bed and it’s like my body is tired but my brain is still at my desk, if that makes sense. I’ll have the laptop closed, lights off, everything, and I’m still mentally checking deadlines and whether I paid tuition and what assignment I’m forgetting.\n\nSometimes it’s stupidly specific, like I’ll remember one spreadsheet cell I might’ve messed up, and then suddenly I’m thinking about work, then my class schedule, then money, then whether I should get up and make a list—but if I get up, then I’m definitely not sleeping. So I just lie there in my apartment for hours.\n\nAnd even when I do fall asleep, it doesn’t feel like I really shut off. I wake up feeling like I was still kind of working in my head all night.\n\nDoctor: If your mind is relatively calm at bedtime, are you still unable to fall asleep?\n\nPatient: I mean… if my mind is calm, maybe I’d sleep better, but honestly that almost never happens lately. Bedtime is sort of when everything starts replaying.\n\nLike I’ll be in bed in my apartment, laptop closed, trying to be “done,” and then I’m rechecking work deadlines in my head, or wondering if I paid tuition, or thinking about class assignments I might’ve missed. And then it turns into hours.\n\nSo I don’t know if I can really answer for a calm night, because the problem is that I don’t really get many of those. Even when I do fall asleep, I wake up feeling like I never actually turned off.\n\nDoctor: Have you been worrying like this on most days for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s definitely been more than six months. I’d say about a year and a few months—maybe a year and three months? It really started when I began that evening graduate certificate program while still working full-time as a management analyst.\n\nAt first I thought it was just because the semester was new and I needed to get organized, like buying the right planner and figuring out which nights were for class stuff versus work stuff. But it just kind of… kept going. So, yes, most days for that whole stretch.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or unable to relax?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate “restless” from just… busy, if that makes sense. I do have a hard time just sitting and relaxing, but then I also feel like everyone I know is kind of like that now—like my roommate will put on a show and still be scrolling and texting three people, so I don’t know what counts as abnormal.\n\nI guess if I try to relax, I’m usually thinking of what else I should be doing. Even on the couch I’ll sort of pop up to check something or move around the apartment. But, yeah, I don’t know if I’d call it restless exactly. What do you mean by unable to relax?\n\nDoctor: On nights when you’re not worrying much, do you still have trouble falling asleep?\n\nPatient: I guess… I don’t really have many nights where I’m not worrying much, so it’s hard to separate out.
